Uniform-in-time estimates on corrections to mean field for interacting Brownian particles
Armand Bernou, Mitia Duerinckx

Abstract
. We consider a system of N N classical Brownian particles interacting via a smooth long-range potential in the mean-field regime, and we analyze the propagation of chaos in form of uniform-in-time estimates with optimal N N -dependence on many-particle correlation functions. Our results cover both the kinetic Langevin setting and the corresponding overdamped Brownian dynamics. The approach is mainly based on so-called Lions expansions, which we combine with new diagrammatic tools to capture many-particle cancellations, as well as with fine ergodic estimates on the linearized mean-field equation, and with discrete stochastic calculus with respect to initial data. In the process, we derive some new ergodic estimates for the linearized Vlasov–Fokker–Planck kinetic equation that are of independent interest. Our analysis also leads to a uniform-in-time quantitative central limit theorem and to concentration estimates for the empirical measure associated with the particle dynamics.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 60K35;
Secondary 35Q84,
35Q70,
60H07,
60F05,
82C22,
82C31

1.
Introduction

1.1.
General overview

We consider the Langevin dynamics for a system of
N
Brownian particles with mean-field interactions, moving in a confining potential in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 1
, as described by the following system of coupled SDEs: for
1\leq i\leq N,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{d}X^{i,N}_{t}=V^{i,N}_{t}\mathrm{d}t,&\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{d}V^{i,N}_{t}=-\tfrac{\kappa}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\nabla W(X^{i,N}_{t}-X_{t}^{j,N})\,\mathrm{d}t-\tfrac{\beta}{2}V^{i,N}_{t}\mathrm{d}t-\nabla A(X^{i,N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{i},&\quad t\geq 0,\\[5.69054pt]
(X^{i,N}_{t},V^{i,N}_{t})|_{t=0}=(X^{i,N}_{\circ},V^{i,N}_{\circ}),\end{array}\right.

where
\{Z^{i,N}:=(X^{i,N},V^{i,N})\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
is the set of particle positions and velocities in the phase space
\mathbb{D}^{d}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
W:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a long-range interaction potential, where
A
is a uniformly convex confining potential, where
\{B^{i}\}_{i}
are i.i.d.
d
-dimensional Brownian motions, and where
\kappa,\beta>0
are given constants.
We assume that the interaction potential
W
satisfies the action-reaction condition
W(x)=W(-x)
,
and that it is smooth,
W\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Regarding the confining potential
A
, we choose it to be quadratic for simplicity,

(1.4)

A(x)\,:=\,\tfrac{1}{2}a|x|^{2},\qquad\text{for some $a>0$},

although this is not essential for our results; see Section
1.3
below.
Next to this Langevin dynamics, we also consider its overdamped limit, that is, the following inertialess Brownian dynamics: for
1\leq i\leq N
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{d}Y^{i,N}_{t}=-\tfrac{\kappa}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\nabla W(Y^{i,N}_{t}-Y_{t}^{j,N})\,\mathrm{d}t-\nabla A(Y^{i,N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{i},\qquad t\geq 0,\\[5.69054pt]
Y^{i,N}_{t}|_{t=0}=Y^{i,N}_{\circ},\end{array}\right.

where
\{Y^{i,N}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
is now the corresponding set of particle positions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For presentation purposes in this introduction, we restrict to the more delicate setting of the Langevin dynamics (
1.1
), but we emphasize that all our results hold in both cases.

In the regime of a large number
N\gg 1
of particles, we turn to a statistical description of the system and consider the evolution of a random ensemble of particles. In terms of a probability density
F^{N}
on the
N
-particle phase space
(\mathbb{D}^{d})^{N}
, the Langevin dynamics (
1.1
) is equivalent to the Liouville equation

(1.7)

\partial_{t}F^{N}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}\cdot\nabla_{x_{i}}F^{N}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\operatorname{div}}_{v_{i}}((\nabla_{v_{i}}+\beta v_{i})F^{N})\\[-8.53581pt]
+\tfrac{\kappa}{N}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\nabla W(x_{i}-x_{j})\cdot\nabla_{v_{i}}F^{N}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla_{x_{i}}A\cdot\nabla_{v_{i}}F^{N}.

Particles are assumed to be exchangeable, which amounts to the symmetry of
F^{N}
in its
N
variables
z_{i}=(x_{i},v_{i})\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,
1\leq i\leq N
. More precisely, we assume for simplicity that particles are initially chaotic, meaning that the initial data
\{Z_{\circ}^{i,N}:=(X^{i,N}_{\circ},V_{\circ}^{i,N})\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
are i.i.d. with some common phase-space density
\mu_{\circ}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
: in other words,
F^{N}
is initially tensorized,

(1.8)

F_{t}^{N}|_{t=0}=\mu_{\circ}^{\otimes N}.

Throughout, we assume that the initial law
\mu_{\circ}
has some stretched exponential moments:

(1.9)

\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}e^{|z|^{\theta}}\mu_{\circ}(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty,\qquad\text{for some $\theta>0$}.

In the large-
N
limit, we aim at an averaged description of the system and we focus on the evolution of a finite number of “typical” particles as described by the marginals of
F^{N}
,

F^{m,N}_{t}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\,:=\,\int_{(\mathbb{D}^{d})^{N-m}}F^{N}_{t}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N})\,\mathrm{d}z_{m+1}\ldots\mathrm{d}z_{N},\qquad 1\leq m\leq N.

In view of Boltzmann’s chaos assumption, correlations between particles are expected to be negligible to leading order, hence the chaotic behavior of initial data would remain approximately satisfied: this is the so-called propagation of chaos,

(1.10)

F^{m,N}_{t}-(F^{1,N}_{t})^{\otimes m}\,\to\,0,\qquad\text{as $N\uparrow\infty$},

for any fixed
m\geq 1
and
t\geq 0
.
If this holds, it automatically implies the validity of the mean-field limit

F^{m,N}_{t}\,\to\,\mu_{t}^{\otimes m},\qquad\text{as\penalty\ $N\uparrow\infty$},

where
\mu_{t}
is the solution of the Vlasov–Fokker–Planck mean-field equation

(1.11)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}\mu+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\mu=\tfrac{1}{2}{\operatorname{div}}_{v}((\nabla_{v}+\beta v)\mu)+(\nabla A+\kappa\nabla W\ast\mu)\cdot\nabla_{v}\mu,\qquad t\geq 0,\\[2.84526pt]
\mu|_{t=0}=\mu_{\circ},\end{array}\right.

with the short-hand notation
\nabla W\ast\mu(x):=\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\nabla W(x-y)\,\mu(y,v)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}v
. This topic has been extensively investigated since the 1990s, starting in particular with
[
56
,
91
]
; see e.g.
[
67
,
26
]
for a review.

On the formal level, corrections to the propagation of chaos and to the mean-field limit are naturally unravelled by means of the BBGKY approach, which goes back to the work of Bogolyubov
[
9
]
. This starts by noting that the Liouville equation (
1.7
) is equivalent to the following hierarchy of coupled equations for marginals: for
1\leq m\leq N
,

(1.12)

\partial_{t}F^{m,N}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}\cdot\nabla_{x_{i}}F^{m,N}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\operatorname{div}}_{v_{i}}((\nabla_{v_{i}}+\beta v_{i})F^{m,N})\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\nabla_{x_{i}}A\cdot\nabla_{v_{i}}F^{m,N}+\kappa\tfrac{N-m}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\nabla W(x_{i}-x_{*})\cdot\nabla_{v_{i}}F^{m+1,N}(\cdot,z_{*})\,\mathrm{d}x_{*}\,\mathrm{d}v_{*}\\[-8.53581pt]
+\tfrac{\kappa}{N}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\nabla W(x_{i}-x_{j})\cdot\nabla_{v_{i}}F^{m,N},

with the convention
F^{m,N}\equiv 0
for
m>N
.
In each of those equations, the last right-hand side term is precisely the one that disrupts the chaotic structure: it creates correlations between initially independent particles, hence leads to deviations from the mean-field approximation. This term is formally of order
O(m^{2}N^{-1})
, and we are actually led to conjecture the following error estimate for the propagation of chaos,

(1.13)

F^{m,N}_{t}-(F^{1,N}_{t})^{\otimes m}\,=\,O(mN^{-1}).

This was first established by Lacker
[
72
]
, see also
[
14
]
, based on a rigorous BBGKY analysis, and it is referred to as estimating the
size of chaos
, cf.
[
4
,
85
]
.
In case of non-Brownian interacting particles, the problem is more difficult as no rigorous BBGKY analysis is available: the rate
O(N^{-1})
for fixed
m
was obtained in
[
43
]
by means of different, non-hierarchical techniques. In the sequel, we systematically leave aside all issues related to the sharp dependence on
m
in such estimates and only focus on optimal convergence rates in
N
for fixed
m
.

A variant of the above estimates
is given by so-called
weak propagation of chaos
estimates: for any sufficiently well-behaved functional
\Phi
defined on the space
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
of probability measures on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
, one expects

(1.14)

\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(\mu_{t}^{N})\right]-\Phi(\mu_{t})\,=\,O(N^{-1}),

in terms of the empirical measure

(1.15)

\textstyle\mu_{t}^{N}:=\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{Z_{t}^{i,N}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \in\penalty\ \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}^{d}),

where we recall that the limit
\mu_{t}
is the solution of the mean-field equation (
1.11
) and where the expectation
\mathbb{E}
is taken with respect to both the initial data and the Brownian forces.
Such an estimate is essentially equivalent to (
1.13
) (leaving aside the dependence on
m
and
\Phi
),
and we refer to
[
69
,
79
,
81
,
7
,
28
]
for results in that direction.
Note that the rate
O(N^{-1})
in (
1.14
) is only expected for
\Phi
smooth enough. For the specific choice
\Phi=\mathcal{W}_{2}(\cdot,\mu_{t})
, for instance, the question amounts to estimating the expectation of the
2
-Wasserstein distance between
\mu_{t}^{N}
and
\mu_{t}
: this is referred to as
strong propagation of chaos
and is known to lead only to a weaker convergence rate
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{t}^{N},\mu_{t})\right]=O(N^{-1/2})
in link with random fluctuations of the empirical measure; see
[
5
,
78
,
12
,
25
,
53
,
72
]
.

In recent years, there has been an increasing interest in
uniform-in-time versions
of the above chaos estimates (
1.13
) or (
1.14
).
This happens to be an important question both in theory and for practical applications: it amounts to describing the long-time behavior of particle systems uniformly in the limit
N\uparrow\infty
, thus showing in particular the proximity of corresponding equilibria.
This is naturally related to the long-time behavior of the mean-field equation (
1.11
), which has itself been
an intense topic of research for more than two decades. While the mean-field equilibrium is not unique in general, cf.
[
47
]
, the long-time convergence of the mean-field density has been established
under several types of assumptions guaranteeing uniqueness
[
95
,
11
,
65
,
82
,
58
,
2
]
;
see also
[
6
,
23
,
10
]
for the Brownian dynamics.
Uniform-in-time propagation of chaos, however, is a more subtle question and is indeed not ensured by the uniqueness of the mean-field equilibrium
[
78
,
8
]
.
In case of small interaction
\kappa\ll 1
, uniform-in-time weak chaos estimates with optimal rate
O(N^{-1})
were first obtained by Delarue and Tse
[
38
]
for the Brownian dynamics, and the uniform-in-time version of (
1.13
) was actually obtained recently in
[
73
]
both for the Langevin and Brownian dynamics.
We also refer to
[
78
,
12
,
25
,
11
,
48
,
87
,
58
,
30
]
for corresponding uniform-in-time
strong
chaos estimates with weaker convergence rates.
Much recent work has focused on weakening the assumptions for the validity of uniform-in-time chaos estimates: we refer in particular to
[
38
, Sections 3.6 and 4]
and references therein for the relaxation of the smallness condition
\kappa\ll 1
, and we refer to
[
60
,
86
,
59
,
32
,
3
]
for singular interactions.

In the present work, while mainly sticking to the simplest case of smooth interactions under the smallness condition
\kappa\ll 1
, we aim to go beyond uniform-in-time chaos estimates by further estimating many-particle correlation functions, which provides finer information on the propagation of chaos in the system. The two-particle correlation function is defined as

G^{2,N}\,:=\,F^{2,N}-(F^{1,N})^{\otimes 2},

which captures the defect to propagation of chaos (
1.10
) at the level of two-particle statistics.
From the BBGKY hierarchy (
1.12
), we note that proving the mean-field limit
F^{1,N}_{t}\to\mu_{t}
amounts to proving
G^{2,N}_{t}\to 0
, which is precisely ensured by standard chaos estimates, cf. (
1.13
).
Yet, two-particle correlations do not allow to reconstruct the full particle density
F^{N}
: in particular, understanding corrections to the mean-field limit requires to further estimate higher-order correlation functions
\{G^{k,N}\}_{2\leq k\leq N}
. Those are defined as suitable polynomial combinations of marginals of
F^{N}
in such a way that the full particle distribution
F^{N}
be recovered in form of a cluster expansion,

(1.16)

F^{N}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N})\,=\,\sum_{\pi\vdash\llbracket N\rrbracket}\prod_{A\in\pi}G^{\sharp A,N}(z_{A}),

where
\pi
runs through the list of all partitions of the index set
\llbracket N\rrbracket:=\{1,\ldots,N\}
, where
A
runs through the list of blocks of the partition
\pi
, where
\sharp A
is the cardinality of
A
, and where for
A=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\}\subset\llbracket N\rrbracket
we write
z_{A}=(z_{i_{1}},\ldots,z_{i_{l}})
. As is easily checked, correlation functions are fully determined by prescribing (
1.16
) together with the “maximality” requirement
\int_{\mathbb{D}}G^{m,N}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\,\mathrm{d}z_{l}=0
for
1\leq l\leq m
. More explicitly, we can write

\displaystyle G^{3,N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{sym}}\big(F^{3,N}-3F^{2,N}\otimes F^{1,N}+2(F^{1,N})^{\otimes 3}\big),

\displaystyle G^{4,N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{sym}}\big(F^{4,N}-4F^{3,N}\otimes F^{1,N}-3F^{2,N}\otimes F^{2,N}+12F^{2,N}\otimes(F^{1,N})^{\otimes 2}-6(F^{1,N})^{\otimes 4}\big),

and so on, where the symbol ‘
{\operatorname{sym}}
’ stands for the symmetrization of coordinates. More generally, for all
2\leq m\leq N
,

(1.17)

G^{m,N}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\,=\,\sum_{\pi\vdash\llbracket m\rrbracket}(\sharp\pi-1)!(-1)^{\sharp\pi-1}\prod_{A\in\pi}F^{\sharp A,N}(z_{A}),

where we use a similar notation as in (
1.16
) and where
\sharp\pi
stands for the number of blocks in a partition
\pi
.
While standard propagation of chaos leads to
G^{2,N}_{t}=O(N^{-1})
, cf. (
1.13
), and in fact
G^{m,N}_{t}=O(N^{-1})
for all
2\leq m\leq N
, a formal analysis of the BBGKY hierarchy (
1.12
) further leads to expect

(1.18)

G_{t}^{m,N}\,=\,O(N^{1-m}),\qquad 2\leq m\leq N.

We refer to this as
higher-order propagation of chaos
:
such estimates provide a much deeper understanding of the structure of propagation of chaos and are key tools to describe deviations from mean-field theory, cf.
[
42
,
43
,
66
,
46
]
.

Estimates of the form (
1.18
) have been obtained in several settings up to an exponential time growth: non-Brownian particle systems were covered in
[
43
]
, while in
[
66
]
the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
) was covered in the more general case of bounded non-smooth interactions.
In the present work, we obtain for the first time corresponding
uniform-in-time
estimates, both for the Langevin and Brownian dynamics.
In fact, after this work was completed, in the simpler case of the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
), similar uniform-in-time estimates have been further obtained in
[
98
]
for bounded non-smooth interactions, thereby improving on our results, but to our knowledge those arguments do not extend easily to the Langevin setting (
1.1
).
Along the way, we establish a quantitative uniform-in-time central limit theorem (CLT) for the empirical measure with an optimal convergence rate, which is new even for the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
), and we also prove uniform-in-time concentration estimates.

From the technical perspective, as described further in Section
1.4
below, we mainly take inspiration from a recent work by Delarue and Tse
[
38
]
,
where the uniform-in-time chaos estimate (
1.14
) was established for the Brownian dynamics. The key idea is to consider the mean-field semigroup induced on functionals
\mu\mapsto\Phi(\mu)
on the space of probability measures, and then to appeal to the so-called Lions calculus on this space to expand the expectation
\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu_{t}^{N})]
of functionals along the particle dynamics; see Lemma
2.1
below. As noted in
[
38
]
, the resulting Lions expansions can be combined with ergodic properties of the linearized mean-field equation to deduce uniform-in-time estimates.
In fact, this whole strategy is the heir to the functional framework originally developed by Mischler and Mouhot
[
79
,
81
]
(also in the context of Kac’s program), where uniform-in-time propagation of chaos was obtained by comparing the particle dynamics with the mean-field semigroup induced directly on the space of measures — rather than on
(nonlinear) functionals on
the space of measures, which is much more convenient for our analysis.
In order to control correlation functions
\{G^{m,N}\}_{2\leq m\leq N}
, we reduce the problem to estimating cumulants of functionals of the empirical measure
\{\kappa_{m}(\Phi(\mu_{t}^{N}))\}_{m\geq 1}
, we apply (iterated) Lions expansions to cumulants, and we develop suitable diagrammatic tools to efficiently capture cancellations in such expansions and derive the desired estimates (
1.18
).
To account for the effect of initial correlations, we further combine Lions expansions with the so-called Glauber calculus that we developed in
[
43
]
.
In order to deduce uniform-in-time estimates, while only the case of the Brownian dynamics was considered in
[
38
]
, we need to further appeal to hypocoercivity techniques to establish the relevant ergodic estimates for the linearized mean-field equation in case of the kinetic Langevin dynamics: for that purpose, we mainly draw inspiration from another work of Mischler and Mouhot
[
80
]
, which we are led to revisit in several ways; see Theorem
2.13
below.

We anticipate that our approach, based on capturing cancellations in Lions expansions for cumulants, and combining such expansions with Stein’s method and Herbst’s argument to further deduce a quantitative CLT and concentration estimates, might also be useful in other contexts, in particular for mean-field games.

1.2.
Main results

We start with the statement of uniform-in-time higher-order propagation of chaos estimates with the optimal
N
-dependence (
1.18
). Note that the smallness condition
\kappa\ll 1
for the interaction strength ensures the uniqueness of the steady state for the mean-field equation (
1.11
), which is key to guarantee strong ergodic properties.

Theorem 1.1

(Uniform-in-time higher-order propagation of chaos)
.

Consider the Langevin dynamics (
1.1
) and the associated correlation functions
\{G^{m,N}\}_{2\leq m\leq N}
as defined in (
1.17
), and assume that the initial law has stretched exponential moments (
1.9
) for some
\theta>0
.
For all
m\geq 2
, there exist
\kappa_{m}>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,W,A,\theta,m
),
C_{m}>0
(further depending on
\mu_{\circ}
), and

\ell_{m}>0
(only depending on
m
),
such that
given
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{m}]

we have for all
N,t\geq 0
,

(1.19)

\|G^{m,N}_{t}\|_{W^{-\ell_{m},1}(\mathbb{D}^{d})^{\otimes m}}\,\leq\,C_{m}N^{1-m}.

Remark 1.2
.

While we focus here on the Langevin dynamics (
1.1
), we recall that all our results also hold in the simpler case of the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
). In that case, the proof is substantially simplified and a slightly stronger version of Theorem
1.1
is actually obtained. On the one hand, the initial law
\mu_{\circ}
only needs to have
some
bounded moment, that is,
\int_{\mathbb{X}}|z|^{p}\mu_{\circ}(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty
for some
p>0
. On the other hand, the smallness condition
\kappa\ll 1
can be taken independently of
m
: there exists
\kappa_{0}>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,W,A
) such that the same result holds for any
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
. As explained in Section
1.3
, this smallness condition can even be removed in some cases.

Let us briefly emphasize some applications of the above correlation estimates. The main one is that these can be used to truncate the BBGKY hierarchy (
1.12
) to an arbitrary precision in
N
.
Of course, the uniform-in-time estimate on two-particle correlations,
G^{2,N}=O(N^{-1})
, allows to recover the uniform-in-time chaos estimate (
1.13
), thus recovering the result of
[
73
]
for smooth interactions (leaving aside the dependence on
m
). More interestingly, the estimate on three-particle correlations allows to further capture the so-called Bogolyubov correction to mean field, for which we obtain the first uniform-in-time result. We state it here for shortness at the level of the
1
-particle density, but this is easily extended to all marginals. Higher-order correlation estimates can be used similarly to capture corrections to an arbitrary order in
N
.

Corollary 1.3

(Bogolyubov correction to mean field)
.

Consider the Langevin dynamics (
1.1
) and assume that the initial law has stretched exponential moments (
1.9
) for some
\theta>0
.
There exist
\kappa_{0}>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,W,A,\theta
),
C_{0}>0
(further depending on
\mu_{\circ}
), and a universal constant
\ell_{0}>0
, such that given
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
we have
for all
N,t\geq 0
,

(1.20)

\displaystyle\|F^{1,N}_{t}-\mu_{t}\|_{W^{-\ell_{0},1}(\mathbb{D}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}N^{-1},

\displaystyle\|F^{1,N}_{t}-\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{N}\|_{W^{-\ell_{0},1}(\mathbb{D}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}N^{-2},

\displaystyle\|NG^{2,N}_{t}-g^{(2)}_{t}\|_{W^{-\ell_{0},1}(\mathbb{D}^{d})^{\otimes 2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}N^{-1},

where we recall that
\mu
is the solution to the Vlasov–Fokker–Planck equation (
1.11
), where
\tilde{\mu}^{N}
satisfies the following corrected version of (
1.11
),

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}\tilde{\mu}^{N}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\tilde{\mu}^{N}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\operatorname{div}}_{v}((\nabla_{v}+\beta v)\tilde{\mu}^{N})+(\nabla A+\kappa\nabla W\ast\tilde{\mu}^{N})\cdot\nabla_{v}\tilde{\mu}^{N}\\[2.84526pt]
\hskip 227.62204pt+\tfrac{\kappa}{N}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\nabla W(x-x_{*})\cdot\nabla_{v}(g^{(2)}-\mu^{\otimes 2})(z,z_{*})\,\mathrm{d}z_{*},\\
\tilde{\mu}^{N}|_{t=0}=\mu_{\circ},\end{array}\right.

and where
g^{(2)}
is the solution of the Bogolyubov equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}g^{(2)}\,=\,L_{\mu}^{(2)}g^{(2)}+\kappa\big(\nabla W(x_{1}-x_{2})-\nabla W\ast\mu(x_{1})\big)\cdot\nabla_{v_{1}}\mu^{\otimes 2}\\[2.84526pt]
\hskip 113.81102pt+\kappa\big(\nabla W(x_{2}-x_{1})-\nabla W\ast\mu(x_{2})\big)\cdot\nabla_{v_{2}}\mu^{\otimes 2},\\
g^{(2)}|_{t=0}=0,\end{array}\right.

where
L_{\mu}^{(2)}:=L_{\mu}\otimes\operatorname{Id}+\operatorname{Id}\otimes L_{\mu}
stands for the
2
-particle linearized Vlasov operator at the mean-field solution
\mu
with

\displaystyle L_{\mu}h\,:=\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\operatorname{div}}_{v}((\nabla_{v}+\beta v)h)-v\cdot\nabla_{x}h+(\nabla A+\kappa\nabla W\ast\mu)\cdot\nabla_{v}h+\kappa(\nabla W\ast h)\cdot\nabla_{v}\mu.

These higher-order corrections to mean-field are precisely correcting chaotic densities by taking into account weak particle correlations that build up over time due to interactions.
On long times, they actually allow to connect the mean-field approximation to Gibbs relaxation. More precisely, it is possible to show that there is
\lambda_{0}>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,W,A
) such that given
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
we have for all
N,t\geq 0
,

(1.21)

\|F_{t}^{1,N}-\mu_{t}-R^{1,N}\|_{W^{-\ell_{0},1}(\mathbb{D}^{d})}\,\leq\,C_{0}e^{-\lambda_{0}t}N^{-1},

where
R^{1,N}:=M^{1,N}-M
is the difference between the first marginal of the
N
-particle Gibbs ensemble and the mean-field equilibrium. Note that this equilibrium correction
R^{1,N}
vanishes in the spatially-homogeneous setting on the torus.
This result (
1.21
) is new in the field and can be viewed as a “cross estimate” combining the mean-field approximation (
1.20
) quantitatively with the convergence of the particle system to Gibbs equilibrium.
Its proof as an application of Theorem
1.1
requires detailed computations of corrections to mean field and is postponed to a forthcoming work.

As the proof of Theorem
1.1
relies on a fine description of cumulants of the empirical measure along the particle dynamics, it can unsurprisingly be pushed further to derive other types of stochastic information on the empirical measure, such as a uniform-in-time quantitative CLT and concentration estimates.
We start with the former.
As expected from formal computations, leading fluctuations of the empirical measure are described by the Gaussian linearized Dean–Kawasaki SPDE, cf. (
2
) below.
A qualitative CLT has actually been known to hold since the early days of mean-field theory
[
92
,
90
,
51
]
, and it has recently been extended to some singular interaction potentials as well
[
97
,
31
]
.
In case of smooth interactions as considered in the present work, an optimal quantitative estimate for fluctuations already follows from
[
43
]
, but we provide here the first uniform-in-time result. To our knowledge, this is new both in the Langevin and Brownian cases.

Theorem 1.4

(Uniform-in-time CLT)
.

Consider the Langevin dynamics (
1.1
) and the associated empirical measure
\mu_{t}^{N}
, cf. (
1.15
), and assume that the initial law has stretched exponential moments (
1.9
) for some
\theta>0
.
There exist
\lambda_{0},\kappa_{0}>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,W,A,\theta
) such that the following holds for any
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
:
for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
, there exists
C_{0}>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,W,A,\mu_{\circ}
) such that we have for all
N,t\geq 0
,

\mathrm{d}_{2}\bigg(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\phi\,(\mu_{t}^{N}-\mu_{t})\,\,;\,\int_{\mathbb{X}}\phi\,\nu_{t}\bigg)\,\leq\,C_{0}\Big(N^{-\frac{1}{2}}+e^{-\lambda_{0}t}N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\Big)\|\phi\|_{W^{4,\infty}(\mathbb{X})}^{2},

where:

—

\mathrm{d}_{2}
stands for the second-order Zolotarev distance between random variables,

(1.22)

\quad\mathrm{d}_{2}(X;Y)\,:=\,\sup\Big\{\mathbb{E}[g(X)]-\mathbb{E}[g(Y)]\penalty\ :\penalty\ g\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ g^{\prime}(0)=0,\penalty\ \|g^{\prime\prime}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}=1\Big\};

—

the limit fluctuation
\nu_{t}
is the centered Gaussian process that is the unique almost sure distributional solution of the Gaussian linearized Dean–Kawasaki SPDE (see Section
5.1
for details),

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}\nu_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\nu_{t}\,=\,{\operatorname{div}}_{v}(\sqrt{\mu_{t}}\xi_{t})+{\operatorname{div}}_{v}((\nabla_{v}+\beta v)\nu_{t})\\[2.84526pt]
\hskip 99.58464pt+\nabla A\cdot\nabla_{v}\nu_{t}+\kappa(\nabla W*\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{v}\mu_{t}+\kappa(\nabla W*\mu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{v}\nu_{t},\\
\nu_{t}|_{t=0}=\nu_{\circ},\end{array}\right.

where
\xi
is a vector-valued space-time white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{D}^{d}
,
and where
\nu_{\circ}
is the Gaussian field describing the fluctuations of the initial empirical measure in the sense that
N^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\phi\,(\mu^{N}_{\circ}-\mu_{\circ})
converges in law to
\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\phi\,\nu_{\circ}
for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
.
1
1

1

In other words, this means that
\nu_{\circ}
is the random tempered distribution on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
characterized by having Gaussian law with
\mathbb{E}[\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\phi\nu_{\circ}]=0
and
\operatorname{Var}[\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\phi\nu_{\circ}]=\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}(\phi-\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\phi\mu_{\circ})^{2}\mu_{\circ}
for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
.

Remark 1.5

(Convergence rate)
.

The convergence rate in the above CLT can be improved to
O(N^{-1/2})
under some non-degeneracy condition. More precisely, the proof shows

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{2}\bigg(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\phi\,(\mu_{t}^{N}-\mu_{t})\,\,;\,\int_{\mathbb{X}}\phi\,\nu_{t}\bigg)\,\leq\,C_{0}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(1+\frac{e^{-3\lambda_{0}t}}{c_{t}(\phi)+N^{-\frac{1}{3}}e^{-\lambda_{0}t}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\phi\|_{W^{4,\infty}(\mathbb{X})}^{2},

in terms of
c_{t}(\phi):=\|\phi\|_{W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{X})}^{-2}\operatorname{Var}[\int_{\mathbb{X}}\phi\tilde{\nu}_{t}]
, where
\tilde{\nu}
is the solution of the linearized mean-field equation with initial condition
\nu_{\circ}
, that is, the solution of the corresponding equation (
2
) with the noise
\xi
replaced by
0
. Under the non-degeneracy condition
c_{t}(\phi)\gtrsim 1
, the rate thus becomes
O(N^{-1/2})
.

Remark 1.6

(Higher-order fluctuations)
.

In recent years, much work has been devoted to the justification of the non-Gaussian nonlinear Dean–Kawasaki equation, which is a highly singular SPDE formally expected to capture higher-order fluctuations; see in particular
[
70
,
33
,
39
,
34
]
.
In contrast, the above result only focuses on
Gaussian leading fluctuations
, but it provides the first uniform-in-time justification.
Extensions to non-Gaussian corrections and the uniform-in-time justification of the nonlinear Dean–Kawasaki equation is postponed to a forthcoming work.

We turn to concentration estimates for the empirical measure, which are easily recovered by combining ideas of the proof of Theorem
1.1
together with Herbst’s argument.
For the Langevin dynamics (
1.1
), the following result complements the concentration estimates obtained in
[
11
, Theorem 5]
(removing the restriction to
r\gg N^{-1/2}+e^{-Ct}
, but requiring more smoothness of the test function
\phi
).
In the simpler case of the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
), on the other hand, corresponding uniform-in-time concentration estimates are already well known: a uniform-in-time concentration estimate was first deduced in
[
77
]
from a logarithmic Sobolev inequality in the case when
W
is convex, which was then largely extended more recently in
[
76
]
. We can also refer to the very recent work of Jackson and Zitridis
[
68
]
where concentration bounds for the Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{p}(\mu^{N}_{t},\mu_{t})
are obtained from relative entropy estimates.

Theorem 1.7

(Uniform-in-time concentration)
.

Consider the Langevin dynamics (
1.1
) and the associated empirical measure
\mu_{t}^{N}
, cf. (
1.15
), and assume for simplicity that the initial law
\mu_{\circ}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
has compact support.
There exists
\kappa_{0}>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,W,A
) such that the following holds for any
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
:
there exists
C_{0}>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,W,A,\mu_{\circ}
) such that we have for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
and
N,t,r\geq 0
,

(1.26)

\mathbb{P}\left[\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\phi\mu^{N}_{t}-\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\phi\mu^{N}_{t}\Big]\geq r\right]\,\leq\,\exp\bigg(-\frac{Nr^{2}}{2C_{0}\|\phi\|_{W^{3,\infty}(\mathbb{D}^{d})}^{2}}\bigg).

Finally, we emphasize that, along our way in this work, we prove a general expansion result for the expectation of smooth functionals of the empirical measure along the particle dynamics, which is of independent interest and extends in particular the work of Chassagneux, Szpruch, and Tse
[
28
]
to the case of the kinetic Langevin dynamics.
More precisely, in the setting of Theorem
1.1
, for all
m\geq 1
and
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{m}]
, for all smooth functionals
\Phi
,
we obtain a truncated expansion of the following form, for all
N,t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})\right]-\Phi(\mu_{t})\,=\,\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{C_{j,\Phi}(t,\mu_{\circ})}{N^{j}}+O(N^{-m-1}),

with exact expressions for the coefficients
\{C_{j,\Phi}(t,\mu_{\circ})\}_{j}
independent of
N
.
As explained in
[
28
, Section 1.1]
, by means of Romberg extrapolation, such an expansion can be used to accelerate the convergence of numerical schemes to estimate
\Phi(\mu_{t})
through the particle method.

1.3.
Extensions

We focus for shortness on the well-traveled setting of interacting Brownian particles with small pairwise interactions derived from a smooth potential in the ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with quadratic confinement. Yet, our analysis is pretty robust and can be adapted as long as suitable ergodic estimates are available for the linearized mean-field equation. We briefly describe several examples.

1.3.1.
Periodic setting

Our analysis is easily adapted to particle systems on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, for instance in the spatially-homogeneous setting
A\equiv 0
, in which case our results still hold in the same form. In the proof, a minor difference appears in the Langevin setting, see Remark
2.14
.
For the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
), thanks to the rich literature on ergodic properties of the linearized mean-field equation on the torus, see e.g.
[
22
,
38
]
, the smallness condition
\kappa\ll 1
can be lifted at least in two cases:

—

Conservative interaction:

If the interaction kernel
\nabla W
is replaced by a smooth force kernel
K\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
with
K(-x)=-K(x)
, and if the force is conservative in the sense of
{\operatorname{div}}K=0
, then we can rely on the ergodic estimates in
[
38
, Section 3.6.2]
and our results hold for the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
) without the restriction
\kappa\ll 1
.

—

‘H-stable’ potential:
If the interaction potential
W\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
has nonnegative Fourier coefficients
\hat{W}(n)\geq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, then we can rely on the ergodic estimates in
[
38
, Section 3.6.3]
and our results hold for the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
) without the restriction
\kappa\ll 1
. In fact, the nonnegativity condition can even be relaxed to
1+2\kappa\inf_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{W}(n)>0
.

1.3.2.
Non-quadratic confinement

Our results also hold in the same form if instead of the quadratic confinement (
1.4
) we choose
A(x)=a|x|^{2}+A^{\prime}(x)
for some
a>0
and some smooth potential
A^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
provided that
\|\nabla^{2}A^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
is small enough (depending on
\beta,W,a
). In that case, we can still appeal to
[
11
]
to ensure the validity of Theorem
2.13
(i) below, while the rest of our approach can be adapted directly without major difficulties.

1.3.3.
More general interactions

Although we focus for shortness on pairwise interactions between the particles, the term
\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\nabla W(X^{i,N}-X^{j,N})
in (
1.1
) (or respectively in (
1.1
)) could be replaced by a more general interaction force of the form
b(X^{i,N},\mu^{N})
for some functional
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where we recall that
\mu^{N}
stands for the empirical measure (
1.15
). While the choice in (
1.1
) amounts to
b(x,\mu)=\nabla W\ast\mu(x)
, our analysis is easily adapted to more general nonlinear functionals
b
under suitable regularity assumptions both on
b
and on its derivatives with respect to
\mu
, together with a corresponding smallness condition on
\kappa
. We skip the details and refer to
[
38
]
for the needed adaptations (see in particular the regularity assumptions (Reg) and (Lip) in
[
38
, Section 2.3.2]
).

1.4.
Strategy and plan of the paper

Let us briefly describe the strategy of the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
It is well known that the estimation of correlation functions
\{G^{m,N}\}_{2\leq m\leq N}
can be reduced to the estimation of cumulants
\{\kappa^{n}(\int_{\mathbb{D}^{d}}\phi\mu_{t}^{N})\}_{n\geq 1}
of linear functionals of the empirical measure
\mu_{t}^{N}
; see Lemma
2.6
.
As the probability space is a product space accounting both for initial data and for Brownian forces, cumulants can be split through the law of total cumulance: we are thus led to consider separately “initial” and “Brownian” cumulants.
To estimate initial cumulants, we appeal to the machinery that we developed in
[
43
]
based on so-called Glauber calculus; see Section
2.3
.
In order to estimate Brownian cumulants, on the other hand, we might try to appeal similarly to Malliavin calculus in the form of
[
83
]
, but this would not easily combine with ergodic properties of the linearized mean-field equation to deduce uniform-in-time estimates.
Instead, we draw inspiration from the recent literature on mean-field games using the master equation formalism and the so-called Lions calculus on the space of probability measures, cf.
[
21
,
19
,
28
,
27
]
. In a nutshell, the idea is to consider the mean-field semigroup induced on functionals
\mu\mapsto\Phi(\mu)
on the space of probability measures, and then use Lions calculus on that space to expand the Brownian expectation
\mathbb{E}_{B}[\Phi(\mu_{t}^{N})]
of functionals along the particle dynamics; see Lemma
2.1
.
Such expansions can be derived explicitly and be pursued to arbitrary order; see also
[
93
]
. In addition, as noted by Delarue and Tse
[
38
]
, they
can be combined with ergodic properties of the linearized mean-field equation to obtain uniform-in-time estimates.
Yet, this does not immediately lead to the desired cumulant estimates
G_{t}^{m,N}=O(N^{1-m})
: for that purpose, we further need to capture underlying cancellations, which we achieve by developing new diagrammatic techniques in form of so-called Lions graphs; see Section
3
.

While we appeal to recent developments on mean-field games for the expansion of
\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})]
along the particle dynamics,
let us emphasize that these techniques are in the same spirit as the functional framework originally developed by Mischler and Mouhot in the context of Kac’s program, interacting diffusions, and jump processes with thermal bath
[
79
,
81
]
, where propagation of chaos was established by comparing the particle dynamics to the mean-field semigroup induced on the space of measures, and where (for the Kac particle system) uniform-in-time estimates were similarly deduced from ergodic properties of the linearized mean-field semigroup.
In the context of mean-field games
[
21
,
19
]
, it has appeared crucial to consider instead the corresponding mean-field semigroup induced on
functionals on
the space of measures, and this more nonlinear setting was conveniently exploited by Delarue and Tse
[
38
]
to prove uniform-in-time chaos estimates (
1.14
) for the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
). In our setting, even though we only consider cumulants of
linear
functionals of the empirical measure, higher-order expansions along the particle dynamics lead us naturally to turn to the same convenient nonlinear framework; see in particular Proposition
3.2
.

When we appeal to the ergodic properties of the linearized mean-field equation to deduce uniform-in-time estimates, some special care is actually needed.
Indeed, while ergodic estimates follow from the standard parabolic theory in the case of the Brownian dynamics, cf.
[
38
]
, we have to further appeal to hypocoercivity techniques in the kinetic Langevin setting. For ergodic estimates on the weighted space
\operatorname{L}^{2}(M^{-1/2})
, where the weight is given by the mean-field steady state
M
, we can simply appeal to hypocoercivity in form of the theory of Dolbeault, Mouhot, and Schmeiser
[
41
]
. For our estimations of cumulants, we rather need ergodic estimates on negative Sobolev spaces, and we easily check that the estimates on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(M^{-1/2})
can be upgraded on
H^{-k}(M^{-1/2})
for all
k\geq 0
.
Yet, we would ideally rather need ergodic estimates on the larger space
W^{-k,1}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
. Unfortunately, even the enlargement theory of Gualdani, Mischler, and Mouhot
[
57
,
80
]
does not allow to reach such spaces. In Section
7
, we revisit enlargement techniques and show that we can actually reach
W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})
with arbitrarily small
q>1
and
p>0
provided
pq^{\prime}\gg 1
, which is of independent interest and is just enough for our purposes.

Finally, in order to deduce the quantitative CLT and the concentration estimates stated in Theorems
1.4
and
1.7
, we combine the same Lions expansions with Stein’s method and with Herbst’s argument, respectively.
We believe this combination of techniques to be of independent interest for applications to other settings, and possibly for mean-field games.
Note that the proof of Theorems
1.4
and
1.7
is actually much simpler than the proof of cumulant estimates in Theorem
1.1
as it does not require to capture arbitrarily fine cancellations. For the quantitative CLT, for instance, the proof essentially boils down to the convergence of the variance and to the smallness of the third cumulant of the empirical measure, thus requiring no fine information on higher cumulants.

Plan of the paper

We start in Section
2
with the presentation of the main technical tools that are used to prove our main results, namely Lions and Glauber calculus, as well as ergodic estimates for the linearized mean-field equation.
In Section
3
, we develop suitable diagrammatic representations for iterated Lions expansions of Brownian cumulants of the empirical measure, which allows us to systematically capture the needed cancellations.
The correlation estimates of Theorem
1.1
are concluded in Section
4
, as well as Corollary
1.3
. The quantitative CLT of Theorem
1.4
is then proved in Section
5
and the concentration estimates of Theorem
1.7
are established in Section
6
.
Finally, Section
7
is devoted to the proof of our new ergodic estimates stated in Section
2
. In Appendix
A
, we include proof details for other preliminary results from Section
2
when necessary.

1.5.
Notation

For notational convenience, we consider a general framework that covers both the Langevin and the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
) and (
1.1
) as special cases.
More precisely, we denote by
\{Z_{t}^{i,N}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
the set of particle trajectories in the space
\mathbb{X}:=\mathbb{D}^{d}
or
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, as given by the following system of coupled SDEs: for
1\leq i\leq N
,

(1.27)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{d}Z^{i,N}_{t}=b(Z^{i,N}_{t},\mu^{N}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}B^{i}_{t},\qquad t\geq 0,\\[2.84526pt]
Z^{i,N}_{t}|_{t=0}=Z_{\circ}^{i,N},\end{array}\right.

where
\mu_{t}^{N}
stands for the empirical measure

\textstyle\mu_{t}^{N}\,:=\,\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{Z_{t}^{i,N}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \in\penalty\ \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X}),

where
b:\mathbb{X}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a smooth functional (in a sense that will be made clear later on), where
\{B^{i}\}_{i}
are i.i.d. Brownian motions in
\mathbb{X}
, and where
\sigma_{0}
is a constant matrix.
We assume that initial data
\{Z_{\circ}^{i,N}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
are i.i.d. with law
\mu_{\circ}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
, and that the latter has streched exponential moments (
1.9
).
The associated mean-field equation takes form of the following McKean–Vlasov equation,

(1.28)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}\mu+{\operatorname{div}}(b(\cdot,\mu)\,\mu)=\tfrac{1}{2}{\operatorname{div}}(a_{0}\nabla\mu),&\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{X}$},\\
\mu|_{t=0}=\mu_{\circ},&\text{in $\mathbb{X}$},\end{array}\right.

with
a_{0}:=\sigma_{0}\sigma_{0}^{T}
, and we denote the well-posed solution operator on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
by

(1.29)

\mu_{t}\,:=\,m(t;\mu_{\circ}).

This general framework allows us to consider both systems of interest (
1.1
) and (
1.1
) at once:
the Langevin dynamics (
1.1
) is given by

(1.30)

\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{D}^{d},\quad b((x,v),\mu)=\big(v,-\tfrac{\beta}{2}v-(\nabla A+\kappa\nabla W\ast\mu)(x)\big),\quad\sigma_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}&0_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\\
0_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}&\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\end{pmatrix},

and the Brownian dynamics (
1.1
) by

(1.31)

\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad b(x,\mu)=-(\nabla A+\kappa\nabla W\ast\mu)(x),\quad\sigma_{0}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}.

Note that the diffusion matrix
a_{0}=\sigma_{0}\sigma_{0}^{T}
is degenerate in the Langevin case, which is why specific hypocoercivity techniques are then needed.
Most of our work can actually be performed in the general framework (
1.27
) without any structural assumption on
\mathbb{X},b,\sigma_{0}
, except when establishing ergodic estimates in Section
7
. More precisely, our different main results hold for any system of the form (
1.27
), under suitable smoothness assumptions for
b
, provided that the ergodic estimates of Theorem
2.13
are available. For the latter, we restrict to the setting of the Langevin or Brownian dynamics in the weak coupling regime
\kappa\ll 1
. Under mere smoothness assumptions on
b
, if ergodic estimates are not available, we note that our analysis can at least be repeated to obtain non-uniform estimates with exponential time growth.

Finally, let us briefly list the main notation used throughout this work:

—

We denote by
C\geq 1
any constant that only depends on the space dimension
d
. We use the notation
\lesssim
(resp.
\gtrsim
) for
\leq C\times
(resp.
\geq\frac{1}{C}\times
) up to such a multiplicative constant
C
. We write
\simeq
when both
\lesssim
and
\gtrsim
hold. We add subscripts to
C,\lesssim,\gtrsim,\simeq
to indicate dependency on other parameters.

—

The underlying probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
splits as a product
(\Omega,\mathbb{P})=(\Omega_{\circ},\mathbb{P}_{\circ})\times(\Omega_{B},\mathbb{P}_{B})
, where the first factor accounts for random initial data and where the second factor accounts for Brownian forces. The space
\Omega^{\circ}
is endowed with the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{\circ}=\sigma(Z^{1,N}_{\circ},\dots,Z^{N,N}_{\circ})
generated by initial data, while
\Omega_{B}
is endowed with the
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma((B^{1}_{t},\dots,B^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0})
. We also denote by
\mathcal{F}^{B}_{t}:=\sigma((B^{1}_{s},\dots,B^{N}_{s})_{0\leq s\leq t})
the Brownian filtration.
We use
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]
and
\kappa^{m}[\cdot]
to denote the expectation and the cumulant of order
m
with respect to
\mathbb{P}
, and we similarly denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[\cdot],\kappa^{m}_{\circ}[\cdot]
and by
\mathbb{E}_{B}[\cdot],\kappa^{m}_{B}[\cdot]
the expectation and cumulants with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\circ}
and
\mathbb{P}_{B}
, respectively.

—

For any two integers
b\geq a\geq 0
, we use the short-hand notation
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket:=\{a,a+1,\dots,b\}
, and in addition for any integer
a\geq 1
we set
\llbracket a\rrbracket:=\llbracket 1,a\rrbracket
.

—

For all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use the notation
\langle z\rangle:=(1+|z|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

—

For a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
, we use the following short-hand notation for its second moment,

(1.32)

Q(\mu)\,:=\,\Big(\int\langle z\rangle^{2}\mu(\mathrm{d}z)\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

2.
Preliminary

This section is devoted to the presentation and development of the main technical tools used in this work. First, we include several probabilistic tools: we start with an account of the master equation formalism and of Lions calculus for functionals on the space of probability measures, then we turn to the relation between correlation functions and cumulants of the empirical measure, and we also recall useful tools from Glauber calculus.
Next, we present two ingredients from kinetic theory: moment estimates for the particle dynamics and, most importantly, new ergodic estimates for the linearized mean-field equation in appropriate weighted Sobolev spaces. The latter is
the key ingredient for our uniform-in-time estimates; its proof
requires quite some developments and is the topic of Section
7
.

2.1.
Lions calculus

We recall several notions of derivatives for continuous functionals on the space
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
of probability measures (endowed with weak convergence), and how they can be used to expand functionals along the particle dynamics.

2.1.1.
Linear derivative

We start with the notion of
linear derivative
, as used for instance by Lions in his course at Collège de France
[
18
]
; see also
[
21
, Chapter 5]
for a slightly different exposition.
A functional
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be continuously differentiable if there exists a continuous map
\frac{\delta{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}
such that, for all
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\mu_{0})-\mathcal{V}(\mu_{1})\,=\,\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\frac{\delta{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu}\big(s\mu_{0}+(1-s)\mu_{1},y\big)\,(\mu_{0}-\mu_{1})(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s,

and we then call
\frac{\delta{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu}
the linear functional derivative of
\mathcal{V}
. This definition holds up to a constant, which we fix by setting

\int_{\mathbb{X}}\frac{\delta{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu}(\mu,y)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\,=\,0,\qquad\text{for all $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})$.}

The denomination “linear derivative” is understood as it is precisely defined to satisfy for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
and
y\in\mathbb{X}
,

(2.2)

\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{V}((1-h)\mu+h\delta_{y})-\mathcal{V}(\mu)}{h}\,=\,\frac{\delta{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu}(\mu,y).

Higher-order linear derivatives are defined by induction: for all integers
p\geq 1
, if the functional
\mathcal{V}
is
p
-times continuously differentiable, we say that it is
(p+1)
-times continuously differentiable if there exists a continuous map
\frac{\delta^{p+1}{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu^{p+1}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\times\mathbb{X}^{p+1}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for all

\mu,\mu^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
and
y\in\mathbb{X}^{p}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{p}{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu^{p}}(\mu,y)-\frac{\delta^{p}{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu^{p}}(\mu^{\prime},y)\,=\,\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\frac{\delta^{p+1}{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu^{p+1}}\big((s\mu+(1-s)\mu^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\big)\,(\mu-\mu^{\prime})(\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}s.

Once again, to ensure the uniqueness of the
(p+1)
th linear functional derivative
\frac{\delta^{p+1}{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu^{p+1}}
, we choose the convention

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{X}}\frac{\delta^{p+1}{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu^{p+1}}(\mu,y_{1},\dots,y_{p+1})\,\mu(\mathrm{d}y_{p+1})\,=\,0,\qquad\text{for all $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})$ and $y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\in\mathbb{X}$.}

2.1.2.
L-derivative

We further recall the notion of so-called
L-derivatives
(or Lions derivatives, or intrinsic derivatives), as developed in
[
75
]
. We refer e.g. to
[
19
, Section 2.2]
for the link to the Otto calculus on Wasserstein space
[
84
,
1
]
. For a continuously differentiable functional
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{R}
, if the map
y\mapsto\frac{\delta{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu}(\mu,y)
is of class
C^{1}
on
\mathbb{X}
, the L-derivative of
\mathcal{V}
is defined as

(2.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{V}(\mu,y)\,:=\,\nabla_{y}\frac{\delta{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu}(\mu,y).

We also define corresponding higher-order derivatives: for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{p}\in\mathbb{X}
, we define, provided that it makes sense,

\displaystyle\partial^{p}_{\mu}\mathcal{V}(\mu,y_{1},\dots,y_{p})\,:=\,\nabla_{y_{1}}\ldots\nabla_{y_{p}}\frac{\delta^{p}{\mathcal{V}}}{\delta\mu^{p}}\big(\mu,y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\big).

2.1.3.
Master equation formalism

In terms of the above calculus on the space
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
of probability measures, we now introduce the so-called master equation formalism to describe the evolution of functionals on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
along the mean-field flow.
For a smooth functional
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{R}
, we define

(2.4)

\displaystyle U(t,\mu)\,:=\,P_{t}\Phi(\mu)\,:=\,\Phi(m(t,\mu)),\qquad t\geq 0,\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X}),

where we recall the notation
m(t,\mu)
for the mean-field solution operator (
1.29
).
This defines a semigroup
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
acting on bounded measurable functionals on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
.
From
[
16
, Theorem 7.2]
, using the regularity of
b
, and assuming corresponding regularity of
\Phi
, we find that
U(t,\mu)
satisfies the following master equation, which is viewed as an evolution equation for functionals on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
,

(2.5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}U(t,\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{X}}\Big[b(x,\mu)\cdot\nabla_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta\mu}(t,\mu,x)+\frac{1}{2}a_{0}:\nabla^{2}_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta\mu}(t,\mu,x)\Big]\mu(\mathrm{d}x),\\[5.69054pt]
U(0,\mu)=\Phi(\mu),\end{array}\right.

where we recall
a_{0}=\sigma_{0}\sigma_{0}^{T}
.

2.1.4.
Expansions along the particle dynamics

We recall the following useful result that allows to expand functionals along the particle dynamics in terms of the corresponding mean-field flow, cf.
[
21
, (5.131)]
or
[
28
, Lemma 2.8]
. (Note that the proof in
[
28
]
only relies on the master equation (
2.5
) and on
[
27
, Proposition 3.1]
, so that in particular there is no uniform ellipticity requirement for the diffusivity
a_{0}=\sigma_{0}\sigma_{0}^{T}
.)

Lemma 2.1

(see
[
21
,
28
]
)
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth functional and let
U(t,\mu)
be defined in (
2.4
).
Then for all
0\leq s\leq t
we have

(2.6)

U(t-s,\mu^{N}_{s})\,=\,U(t,\mu^{N}_{0})\,+\,\frac{1}{2N}\int_{0}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\operatorname{tr}\Big[a_{0}\,\partial^{2}_{\mu}U(t-u,\mu^{N}_{u})(z,z)\Big]\,\mu^{N}_{u}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\mathrm{d}u\,+\,M^{N}_{t,s},

where
(M^{N}_{t,s})_{s\geq 0}
is a square-integrable
(\mathcal{F}^{B}_{s})_{s\geq 0}
-martingale with
M^{N}_{t,0}=0
, which is explicitly given by

M^{N}_{t,s}\,:=\,\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{s\wedge t}\partial_{\mu}U(t-u,\mu_{u}^{N})(Z^{i,N}_{u})\cdot\sigma_{0}\,\mathrm{d}B^{i}_{u}.

This expansion will be used throughout this work to compare the empirical measure to the corresponding mean-field semigroup. More precisely, we shall abundantly use the following immediate consequences.

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth functional and let
U(t,\mu)
be defined in (
2.4
).

(i)

For all
t\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\qquad\big|\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu_{t}^{N})]-\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[\Phi(m(t,\mu^{N}_{0}))]\big|\,\lesssim\,N^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\big|\partial^{2}_{\mu}U(t-u,\mu_{u}^{N})(z,z)\big|\,\mu^{N}_{u}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\mathrm{d}u\bigg].

(ii)

For all
t\geq 0
, we have

\qquad\|\Phi(\mu_{t}^{N})-\Phi(m(t,\mu_{0}^{N}))\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{B})}\,\lesssim\,N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathbb{E}_{B}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\big|\partial_{\mu}U(t-u,\mu^{N}_{u})(z)\big|^{2}\,\mu_{u}^{N}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\mathrm{d}u\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
+N^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}_{B}\bigg[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\big|\partial_{\mu}^{2}U(t-u,\mu^{N}_{u})(z,z)\big|\,\mu_{u}^{N}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\mathrm{d}u\Big)^{2}\bigg].

Proof.

Taking the expectation
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{\circ}\mathbb{E}_{B}
in (
2.6
), using
\mathbb{E}_{B}[M_{t,s}^{N}]=0
, and setting
s=t
, we are led in particular to the following expansion for the expectation of a functional of the empirical measure,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu_{t}^{N})]\,=\,\mathbb{E}_{\circ}\big[\Phi(m(t,\mu^{N}_{0}))\big]\,+\,\frac{1}{2N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{X}}\operatorname{tr}\Big[a_{0}\,\partial^{2}_{\mu}U(t-u,\mu^{N}_{u})(z,z)\Big]\,\mu^{N}_{u}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg]\mathrm{d}u,

and item (i) immediately follows.
Next, taking the
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{B})
norm in (
2.6
), noting that Jensen’s inequality yields

\qquad\mathbb{E}_{B}[(M_{t,s}^{N})^{2}]\,\leq\,N^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{B}\bigg[\int_{0}^{s\wedge t}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\big|\sigma_{0}^{T}(\partial_{\mu}U)(t-u,\mu_{u}^{N})(z)\big|^{2}\,\mu_{u}^{N}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\mathrm{d}u\bigg],

and setting
s=t
, we similarly obtain item (ii).
∎

Due to the above result, as emphasized in
[
21
,
28
,
38
]
, Lions calculus provides a natural starting point to study propagation of chaos, which was indeed successfully used in particular in
[
38
]
to establish uniform-in-time weak chaos estimates for the Brownian dynamics.
More precisely, in order to obtain weak chaos estimates of the form (
1.14
), that is,

\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu_{t}^{N})]-\Phi(m(t,\mu_{\circ}))\,=\,O(N^{-1}),

we can appeal to item (i) above and it remains to compare
\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[\Phi(m(t,\mu^{N}_{0}))]
to
\Phi(m(t,\mu_{\circ}))
. The missing estimate is provided by the following general result due to
[
28
, Theorem 2.11]
.
We emphasize that it provides some
O(N^{-1})
convergence rate in the law of large numbers for the empirical measure associated with i.i.d. data in some weak topology:
this contrasts with the much weaker rates that are sharply obtained for the expectation of Wasserstein distances e.g. in
[
53
,
52
]
.

Lemma 2.3

(see
[
28
]
)
.

For any smooth functional
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu_{0}^{N})]-\Phi(\mu_{\circ})\,=\,\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\delta^{2}\Phi}{\delta\mu^{2}}(\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,u,z},z,z)-\frac{\delta^{2}\Phi}{\delta\mu^{2}}(\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,u,z},z,Z_{\circ}^{1,N})\bigg]\,\mu_{\circ}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\mathrm{d}u\,s\,\mathrm{d}s,

in terms of

\tilde{\mu}_{s,u,z}^{N}:=\frac{su}{N}(\delta_{z}-\delta_{Z_{\circ}^{1,N}})+\mu_{\circ}+s(\mu_{0}^{N}-\mu_{\circ})
.
In particular, this implies

|\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu_{0}^{N})]-\Phi(\mu_{\circ})|\,\leq\,2N^{-1}\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})}\Big\|\frac{\delta^{2}\Phi}{\delta\mu^{2}}\Big\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{X}^{2})}.

2.2.
Correlation functions and cumulants

In order to estimate the many-particle correlation functions
\{G^{k,N}\}_{1\leq k\leq N}
defined in (
1.17
), we shall proceed by estimating the cumulants of the empirical measure, which have a more exploitable probabilistic content.
We recall that the
m
th cumulant of a bounded random variable
X
is defined by

\kappa^{m}[X]:=\Big((\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t})^{m}\log\mathbb{E}\big[e^{tX}\big]\Big)\Big|_{t=0},

that is,

\displaystyle\kappa^{1}[X]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X],

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}[X]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}=\operatorname{Var}[X],

\displaystyle\kappa^{3}[X]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X^{3}]-3\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]\mathbb{E}[X]+2\mathbb{E}[X]^{3},

(2.7)

\displaystyle\kappa^{4}[X]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X^{4}]-4\mathbb{E}[X^{3}]\mathbb{E}[X]-3\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]^{2}+12\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}-6\mathbb{E}[X]^{4},

and so on (see (
A.1
) for a general formula).
We also define the joint cumulant of a family of bounded random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
as

\displaystyle\kappa^{m}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}]\,:=\,\Big(\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}t_{1}\dots\mathrm{d}t_{m}}\log\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\sum_{j=1}^{m}t_{j}X_{j}}\big]\Big)\Big|_{t_{1}=\ldots=t_{m}=0}.

Since we consider in this work a product probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{P})=(\Omega_{\circ},\mathbb{P}_{\circ})\times(\Omega_{B},\mathbb{P}_{B})
, where the first factor accounts for random initial data and the second for Brownian forces,
we appeal to the following law of total cumulance in order to split cumulants accordingly.

Lemma 2.4

(see
[
15
]
)
.

For all
m\geq 2
and all bounded random variables
X
, we have

\kappa^{m}[X]\,=\,\sum_{\pi\vdash\llbracket m\rrbracket}\kappa_{\circ}^{\sharp\pi}\Big[\big(\kappa^{\sharp A}_{B}[X]\big)_{A\in\pi}\Big],

where we recall that
\kappa_{\circ}
and
\kappa_{B}
stand for cumulants with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\circ}
and
\mathbb{P}_{B}
, respectively, and where we use similar notation as in (
1.16
).

2.2.1.
Moments and cumulants

While cumulants are defined as polynomial expressions involving moments, cf. (
2.7
), those relations are easily inverted: similarly as in (
1.16
), moments can be recovered from cumulants in form of a cluster expansion,

(2.8)

\mathbb{E}[X^{m}]\,=\,\sum_{\pi\vdash\llbracket m\rrbracket}\prod_{A\in\pi}\kappa^{\sharp A}[X].

For later purposes, we state the following recurrence relation between moments and cumulants: it immediately implies the above cluster expansion by induction, and it will be useful in this form in the sequel. A short proof is included in Appendix
A
for convenience.

Lemma 2.5
.

For all
m\geq 2
and all bounded random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
, we have

(2.9)

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}\dots X_{m}]\,=\,\sum_{J\subset\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket}\kappa[X_{1},X_{J}]\,\mathbb{E}\bigg[\penalty\ \prod_{j\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket\setminus J}\penalty\ X_{j}\bigg],

where we use the standard convention
\prod_{j\in\varnothing}X_{j}=1
for the empty product.
In particular, for all
m\geq 1
and all bounded random variables
X
, we have

\mathbb{E}[X^{m}]\,=\,\sum_{j=1}^{m}\binom{m-1}{j-1}\kappa^{j}[X]\,\mathbb{E}[X^{m-j}].

2.2.2.
From cumulants to correlations

We work out the standard link between cumulants of the empirical measure and correlation functions.
We state it in form of an inequality that can be directly iterated to bound successive correlation functions in terms of cumulants of the empirical mesure.
This was used for instance in
[
43
, Section 4]
, but we provide a self-contained statement, as well as a short proof in Appendix
A
for convenience.

Lemma 2.6
.

For all
1\leq m\leq N
, there exists
C_{m}>0
such that for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{X})
and
t\geq 0
, we have

\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{X}^{m}}\phi^{\otimes m}G^{m,N}_{t}\bigg|\,\leq\,\bigg|\kappa^{m}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{X}}\phi\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{N}_{t}\bigg]\bigg|\\
+C_{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\vdash\llbracket m\rrbracket\\
\sharp\pi<m\end{subarray}}\sum_{\rho\vdash\pi}N^{\sharp\pi-\sharp\rho-m+1}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{X}^{\sharp\pi}}\Big(\bigotimes_{B\in\pi}\phi^{\sharp B}\Big)\Big(\bigotimes_{D\in\rho}G^{\sharp D,N}_{t}(z_{D})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}z_{\pi}\bigg|.

2.3.
Glauber calculus

We recall some useful tools from the so-called Glauber calculus on
(\Omega_{\circ},\mathbb{P}_{\circ})
as developed by the second-named author in
[
43
]
(see also
[
36
,
45
]
).
Given a random variable
X\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ})
,
we then define its Glauber derivative at
j\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
as

D^{\circ}_{j}X\,:=\,X-\mathbb{E}\big[X\big|(Z_{\circ}^{i,N})_{i:i\neq j}\big].

The full gradient
D^{\circ}X=(D^{\circ}_{j}X)_{j\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
is viewed as an element of
\ell^{2}(\llbracket N\rrbracket;\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ}))
.
As the initial data
(Z_{\circ}^{j,N})_{1\leq j\leq N}
are i.i.d.,
a straightforward computation shows that
D^{\circ}_{j}
is self-adjoint on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ})
and satisfies

D^{\circ}_{j}D^{\circ}_{j}=D^{\circ}_{j},\qquad D^{\circ}_{j}D^{\circ}_{k}=D^{\circ}_{k}D^{\circ}_{j},\qquad\text{for all $j,k\in\llbracket N\rrbracket$}.

We then define the Glauber Laplacian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\circ}\,:=\,(D^{\circ})^{*}D^{\circ}\,=\,\sum_{j=1}^{N}(D^{\circ}_{j})^{*}D^{\circ}_{j}\,=\,\sum_{j=1}^{N}D^{\circ}_{j},

which is a nonnegative self-adjoint operator on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ})
.
We recall some fundamental properties of this operator; see
[
43
, Lemmas 2.5 and 2.6]
.

Lemma 2.7

(see
[
43
]
)
.

(i)

The kernel of
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
is reduced to constants,
\ker\mathcal{L}_{\circ}=\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
has a unit spectral gap above
0
, and its spectrum is the set
\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

The restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
to
(\ker\mathcal{L}_{\circ})^{\bot}=\{X\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ}):\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[X]=0\}
admits a well-defined inverse
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}^{-1}
, which is a nonnegative self-adjoint contraction on
(\ker\mathcal{L}_{\circ})^{\bot}
.
Moreover, this inverse operator satisfies for all
1<p<\infty
and
X\in\operatorname{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\circ})
with
\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[X]=0
,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}_{\circ}^{-1}X\|_{\operatorname{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\circ})}\,\lesssim\,\tfrac{p^{2}}{p-1}\|X\|_{\operatorname{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\circ})}.

(iii)

The following Helffer-Sjöstrand representation holds for covariances: for all
X,Y\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ})
,

(2.11)

\operatorname{Cov}_{\circ}[X,Y]\,=\,\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\big[(D^{\circ}_{j}X)\mathcal{L}_{\circ}^{-1}(D^{\circ}_{j}Y)\big].

Combining the spectral gap for
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
and the Helffer–Sjöstrand inequality (
2.11
), we recover in particular the following well-known variance inequality due to Efron and Stein
[
50
]
: for all
X\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ})
,

(2.12)

\operatorname{Var}_{\circ}[X]\,\leq\,\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[|D^{\circ}_{j}X|^{2}].

2.3.1.
Cumulant estimates via Glauber calculus

As shown in
[
43
, Theorem 2.2]
,
cumulants can be controlled in terms of higher-order Glauber derivatives, which can be viewed as a higher-order version of Poincaré’s inequality (
2.12
) on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ})
with respect to Glauber calculus.
We claim that this can also be extended to the multivariate case, that is, to joint cumulants of families of random variables;
a proof is included in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 2.8
.

For all
n\geq 0
and bounded
\sigma((Z_{\circ}^{j,N})_{1\leq j\leq N})
-measurable random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}
, we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{\circ}^{n+1}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}]\,\lesssim_{n}\,\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}N^{k+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},\dots,a_{n+1}\geq 1\\
\sum_{j}a_{j}=n+k+1\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\big\|(D^{\circ})^{a_{j}}X_{j}\big\|_{\ell^{\infty}_{\neq}\big(\operatorname{L}^{\frac{1}{a_{j}}(n+k+1)}(\Omega_{\circ})\big)},

where we have set

\big\|(D^{\circ})^{m}Z\|_{\ell^{\infty}_{\neq}(\operatorname{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\circ}))}\,:=\,\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\dots j_{m}\\
\text{distinct}\end{subarray}}\big\|D^{\circ}_{j_{1}}\dots D^{\circ}_{j_{m}}Z\big\|_{\operatorname{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\circ})}.

2.3.2.
Asymptotic normality via Glauber calculus

As the approximate normality of a random variable essentially follows from the smallness of its cumulants of order
\geq 3
, there is no surprise that it can be quantified as well by means of Glauber calculus. The following result is typically known in the literature as a “second-order Poincaré inequality” for approximate normality. It was first established by Chatterjee
[
29
, Theorem 2.2]
based on Stein’s method for the
1
-Wasserstein distance, while the corresponding bound on the Kolmogorov distance is due to
[
71
, Theorem 4.2]
. For our purposes in this work, we claim that the same result also holds for the Zolotarev distance; a short proof is included in Appendix
A
for convenience.

Proposition 2.9

(Second-order Poincaré inequality
[
29
,
71
]
)
.

For all bounded
\sigma((Z_{\circ}^{j,N})_{j})
-measurable random variable
Y
, setting
\sigma_{Y}^{2}:=\mathrm{Var}\left[Y\right]
, there holds

\mathrm{d}_{2}\bigg(\frac{Y-\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[Y]}{\sigma_{Y}};\mathcal{N}\bigg)+\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{W}}\left({\frac{Y-\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[Y]}{\sigma_{Y}}};{\mathcal{N}}\right)+\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{K}}\left({\frac{Y-\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[Y]}{\sigma_{Y}}};{\mathcal{N}}\right)\\
\,\lesssim\,\frac{1}{\sigma_{Y}^{3}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[|D_{j}^{\circ}Y|^{6}]^{\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{Y}^{2}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Big(\sum_{l=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[|D_{l}^{\circ}Y|^{4}]^{\frac{1}{4}}\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[|D_{j}^{\circ}D_{l}^{\circ}Y|^{4}]^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big)^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{W}}\left({\cdot};{\mathcal{N}}\right)
and
\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{K}}\left({\cdot};{\mathcal{N}}\right)
stand for the
1
-Wasserstein and the Kolmogorov distances
2
2

2

Recall that for two real-valued random variables
X,Y
the
1
-Wasserstein and Kolmogorov distances are defined as

\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{W}}\left({X};{Y}\right)=\sup\big\{\mathbb{E}[g(X)]-\mathbb{E}[g(Y)]:g\in C(\mathbb{R}),\,\operatorname{Lip}(g)\leq 1\big\},\qquad\operatorname{d}_{\operatorname{K}}\left({X};{Y}\right)=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{P}[X\leq t]-\mathbb{P}[Y\leq t]|.

to a standard Gaussian random variable
\mathcal{N}
, respectively, and where we recall that
\mathrm{d}_{2}(\cdot;\mathcal{N})
stands for the corresponding second-order Zolotarev distance defined in (
1.22
).

2.3.3.
Concentration via Glauber calculus

We show the following concentration estimate for random variables in
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ})
. It follows from some degraded version of a log-Sobolev inequality, combined with Herbst’s argument.
Note however that we do not have an exact log-Sobolev inequality with respect to Glauber calculus, cf.
[
74
]
, which is why we need to require an
almost sure
a priori bound on the Glauber derivative. The proof is postponed to Appendix
A
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\circ})
be
\sigma((Z^{j,N}_{\circ})_{1\leq j\leq N})
-measurable with
\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[X]=0
and
|D^{\circ}_{j}X|\leq\frac{1}{2}L
almost surely for all
1\leq j\leq N
, for some constant
L>0
.
Then for all
\lambda>0
we have

\mathbb{E}_{\circ}[e^{\lambda X}]\,\leq\,\exp\Big(\tfrac{N}{2}\lambda L(e^{\lambda L}-1)\Big).

In particular, this entails

\mathbb{P}_{\circ}[X>r]\,\leq\,\exp\Big(-\tfrac{r}{4L}\log\big(1+\tfrac{r}{NL}\big)\Big),

where the right-hand side is
\leq\exp(-\frac{r^{2}}{8NL^{2}})
as long as
r\leq NL
.

2.3.4.
Link to linear derivatives

As the following lemma shows, Glauber derivatives can be estimated in terms of linear derivatives. This is particularly convenient in the sequel to unify notations when both Glauber and linear derivatives are involved.

Lemma 2.11
.

Given a smooth functional
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{R}
,
we have almost surely for all
k\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
and all distinct indices
j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
,

\big|D_{j_{1}}^{\circ}\dots D_{j_{k}}^{\circ}\Phi(\mu^{N}_{0})\big|\,\leq\,N^{-k}\,2^{k}\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})}\Big\|\frac{\delta^{k}{\Phi}}{\delta\mu^{k}}(\mu,\cdot)\Big\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{X}^{k})}.

Proof.

For all
j\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, by definition of the Glauber derivative and of the linear derivative, we can compute

(2.13)

\displaystyle D_{j}^{\circ}\Phi(\mu^{N}_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi(\mu^{N}_{0})-\int_{\mathbb{X}}\Phi\Big(\mu_{0}^{N}+\tfrac{1}{N}(\delta_{z}-\delta_{Z_{0}^{j,N}})\Big)\,\mu_{\circ}(\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{-1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta\mu}\Big(\mu_{0}^{N}+\tfrac{1-s}{N}(\delta_{z}-\delta_{Z_{0}^{j,N}})\,,\,y\Big)\,(\delta_{Z_{0}^{j,N}}-\delta_{z})(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mu_{\circ}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\mathrm{d}s.

By induction, we are led to the following representation formula for iterated Glauber derivatives: for all
k\geq 1
and all distinct indices
j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
,

(2.14)

D_{j_{1}}^{\circ}\ldots D_{j_{k}}^{\circ}\Phi(\mu^{N}_{0})\,=\,N^{-k}\int_{([0,1]\times\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X})^{k}}\frac{\delta^{k}{\Phi}}{\delta\mu^{k}}\bigg(\mu_{0}^{N}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tfrac{1-s_{i}}{N}(\delta_{z_{i}}-\delta_{Z_{0}^{j_{i},N}})\,,\,y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}\bigg)\\[-5.69054pt]
\times\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\delta_{Z^{j_{i},N}_{0}}-\delta_{z_{i}})(\mathrm{d}y_{i})\,\mu_{\circ}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\,\mathrm{d}s_{i},

and the conclusion follows.
∎

2.4.
Moment estimates on particle dynamics

The following uniform-in-time moment estimates are stated both for the particle dynamics and for its mean-field counterpart.
The proof easily follows along the lines of the analysis in
[
11
]
and is postponed to Appendix
A
. Recall the notation
Q
from (
1.32
).

Lemma 2.12
.

There exist
\lambda,C>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,a
,
\|\nabla W\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
) such that the following moment estimates hold.

(i)

Algebraic moments:

For all
t\geq 0
,
N,k\geq 1
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
, we have

(2.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{B}\big[Q(\mu_{t}^{N})^{k}\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(Ck)^{\frac{k}{2}}(1+\tfrac{k}{N})^{\frac{k}{2}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{X}}\langle e^{-\lambda t}z\rangle^{2}\,\mu^{N}_{0}(\mathrm{d}z)\Big)^{\frac{k}{2}},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{X}}|z|^{k}\,m(t,\mu)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(Ck)^{k}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\langle e^{-\lambda t}z\rangle^{k}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}z).

(ii)

Subgaussian moments:

For all
t,\alpha\geq 0
,
N,k\geq 1
,
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
, and
0\leq\delta<2
, we have

(2.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{B}\big[e^{\alpha Q(\mu_{t}^{N})^{\delta}}\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp\Big(C_{\delta}\alpha(\tfrac{\alpha}{N}+1)^{\frac{\delta}{2-\delta}}\Big)\exp\bigg(C\alpha\Big(\int_{\mathbb{X}}|e^{-\lambda t}z|^{2}\mu^{N}_{0}(\mathrm{d}z)\Big)^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\bigg),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{X}}e^{\alpha|z|^{\delta}}m(t,\mu)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp\Big(C_{\delta}\alpha^{\frac{2}{2-\delta}}\Big)\int_{\mathbb{X}}e^{C\alpha|e^{-\lambda t}z|^{\delta}}\mu(\mathrm{d}z),

for some constant
C_{\delta}
only depending on
d,\beta,a,\delta
,
\|\nabla W\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

(iii)

Gaussian moments:

For all
t\geq 0
,
N,k\geq 1
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
, we have for all
0\leq\alpha\leq\frac{1}{C}N
,

(2.17)

\mathbb{E}_{B}\big[e^{\alpha Q(\mu_{t}^{N})^{2}}\big]\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ C\exp\Big(C\alpha\int_{\mathbb{X}}|e^{-\lambda t}z|^{2}\mu_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}z)\Big),

and for all
0\leq\alpha\leq\frac{1}{C}
,

\int_{\mathbb{X}}e^{\alpha|z|^{2}}\,m(t,\mu)\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ C\int_{\mathbb{X}}e^{C\alpha|e^{-\lambda t}z|^{2}}\mu(\mathrm{d}z).

2.5.
Ergodic estimates for mean field

The last main ingredient takes form of ergodic estimates for the linearized mean-field equation, which is indeed the key tool for our uniform-in-time estimates in the spirit of
[
38
]
.
Given
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{X})
, the linearized mean-field McKean–Vlasov operator at
\mu

is defined as follows: for all
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{X})
with
\int_{\mathbb{X}}h=0
,

(2.18)

\displaystyle L_{\mu}h\,:=\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\operatorname{div}}(a_{0}\nabla h)-{\operatorname{div}}\big(b(\cdot,\mu)h\big)-{\operatorname{div}}\Big(\mu\int_{\mathbb{X}}\frac{\delta{b}}{\delta\mu}(\cdot,\mu,z)\,h(z)\,\mathrm{d}z\Big).

In the Langevin setting (
1.30
), this means for all
h
on
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(2.19)

L_{\mu}h\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\operatorname{div}}_{v}((\nabla_{v}+\beta v)h)-v\cdot\nabla_{x}h+(\nabla A+\kappa\nabla W\ast\mu)\cdot\nabla_{v}h+\kappa(\nabla W\ast h)\cdot\nabla_{v}\mu,

and in the Brownian setting (
1.31
), this means for all
h
on
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

L_{\mu}h\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\triangle h+{\operatorname{div}}(h\nabla A)+\kappa{\operatorname{div}}(h\nabla W\ast\mu)+\kappa{\operatorname{div}}(\mu\nabla W\ast h).

For our purposes in this work, we shall establish ergodic estimates in a weighted Sobolev framework with arbitrary integrability, negative regularity, and polynomial weight: more precisely,
for all
1\leq q\leq 2
and
p\geq 0
, we consider the space
\operatorname{L}^{q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})
as the weighted Lebesgue space with the norm

\|h\|_{\operatorname{L}^{q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})}\,:=\,\|\langle z\rangle^{p}h\|_{\operatorname{L}^{q}(\mathbb{X})}\,=\,\Big(\int_{\mathbb{X}}|h(z)|^{q}\,\langle z\rangle^{pq}\,\mathrm{d}z\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}},

and, for all
k\geq 0
, we consider the space
W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})
as the weighted negative Sobolev space associated with the dual norm

(2.20)

\|h\|_{W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})}\,:=\,\sup\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{X}}hh^{\prime}\langle z\rangle^{p}\,:\,\|h^{\prime}\|_{W^{k,q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{X})}=1\bigg\},

where
q^{\prime}:=\frac{q}{q-1}
is the dual integrability exponent and where
W^{k,q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{X})
is the standard Sobolev space with norm

\|h\|_{W^{k,q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{X})}\,:=\,\sup_{0\leq j\leq k}\|\nabla^{j}h\|_{\operatorname{L}^{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{X})}.

In these terms, our ergodic estimates take on the following guise.
While item (i) is well known (see e.g.
[
11
]
and the discussion below), our main contribution is to prove the Sobolev estimates of item (ii).
Note that the restriction
pq^{\prime}\gg 1
in the Langevin setting is fairly natural: indeed, we note for example that the restriction
pq^{\prime}>d
precisely ensures that the spatial density
\rho_{h}(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,v)\,\mathrm{d}v
is defined in
\operatorname{L}^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
h\in W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})
.

Theorem 2.13
.

There exist
\kappa_{0},\lambda_{0}>0
(only depending on
d,\beta,a,\|W\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
)
such that the following results hold for any
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]
.

(i)

There is a unique steady state
M
for the mean-field evolution (
1.28
), and the solution operator (
1.29
) satisfies for all
t\geq 0
,

(2.21)

\mathcal{W}_{2}\big(m(t,\mu_{\circ}),M\big)\,\lesssim_{W,\beta,a}\,e^{-\lambda_{0}t}\,\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{\circ},M),

where
\mathcal{W}_{2}
stands for the
2
-Wasserstein distance and where the multiplicative constant only depends on
d,\beta,a
, and
\|W\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

(ii)

Given
1<q\leq 2
and
0<p\leq 1
with
pq^{\prime}\gg_{\beta,a}1
large enough (only depending on
d,\beta,a
), for all
k\geq 2
and all
f_{\circ}\in W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})
with
\int_{\mathbb{X}}f_{\circ}=0
, there is a unique solution
f\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p}))
to the Cauchy problem

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}f_{t}=L_{m(t,\mu_{\circ})}f_{t},\\
f_{t}|_{t=0}=f_{\circ},\end{array}\right.

and it satisfies for all
t\geq 0
,

(2.24)

\|f_{t}\|_{W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})}\,\lesssim_{W,\beta,k,p,q,a,\mu_{\circ}}\,e^{-p\lambda_{0}t}\|f_{\circ}\|_{W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})},

where the multiplicative constant only depends on
d,\beta,k,p,q,a,\|W\|_{W^{k+d+1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, and
Q(\mu_{\circ})
(recall the notation (
1.32
)).
In addition, the dependence on
Q(\mu_{\circ})
can be made at most exponential provided that
\kappa
is small enough. More precisely, given
\theta\in(0,1]
, for all
k\geq 2
and
0<p\leq\frac{1}{4}\theta
, there is some
\kappa_{k,p}\in(0,\kappa_{0}]
(only depending on
d,\beta,\theta,k,p,a
,
\|W\|_{W^{d+3,\infty}\cap W^{k+2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
) such that the following holds provided
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{k,p}]
: given
1<q\leq 2
with
pq^{\prime}\gg_{\beta,a}1
large enough (only depending on
d,\beta,a
), we have for all
t\geq 0
,

(2.25)

\|f_{t}\|_{W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})}\,\lesssim_{W,\beta,k,p,q,a}\,e^{pQ(\mu_{\circ})^{\theta}}e^{-p\lambda_{0}t}\|f_{\circ}\|_{W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})},

where the multiplicative constant now only depends on
d,\beta,k,p,q,a
,
\|W\|_{W^{k+d+1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

The convergence to equilibrium stated in item (i) is well known: it was proven for instance by Bolley, Guillin, and Malrieu
[
11
]
by an elementary coupling argument
(see also
[
77
,
63
,
13
]
for earlier results).
For corresponding results relying on convexity rather than on smallness of the interaction, we refer to
[
82
,
61
]
in the Langevin setting, and to
[
77
,
78
,
23
,
24
,
25
]
in the Brownian setting.
Perturbations of the strictly convex case have also been investigated e.g. in
[
10
,
17
,
49
]
.

Regarding the ergodic estimates stated in item (ii), in the Brownian setting,
they easily follow from classical parabolic theory
[
54
,
55
]
. In the periodic case with
A\equiv 0
, such estimates can be found in
[
20
, Lemma 7.4]
on the space
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and in
[
38
]
on the space
W^{-k,1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with
0\leq k<2
. Those results are easily generalized to the case of a nontrivial confinement in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and they can be checked to hold on the space
W^{-k,q}(\langle z\rangle^{p})
for all
k\geq 1
,
1\leq q\leq 2
, and
0\leq p\leq 1
. We emphasize in particular that they also hold on the unweighted space
W^{-k,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
k\geq 1
, in which case the dependence on
Q(\mu_{\circ})
in (
2.24
) can be completely lifted.
We skip the details as it is similar to
[
38
]
.
Note that the control of higher-order correlation functions indeed requires ergodic estimates in Sobolev spaces with
arbitrary
negative regularity
k\geq 1
.

The main challenge is to obtain the corresponding ergodic estimates in the kinetic Langevin setting, where parabolic tools are no longer available due to hypocoercivity.
This has been a very active area of research over the last two decades.
